===== PDF page_01
SYLLABUS

Unit I: Introduction 06 Hours

Introduction to information retrieval, Major challenges in IR, Features of an IR system, components of an IR model, IR
system block diagram, Boolean retrieval, Information versus Data Retrieval, Text categorization, IR processes and fields,
Vector Model, Probabilistic Model and Latent Semantic Indexing Model.

Case Studies: Visualization and Information Retrieval Techniques for Network Intrusién Detection.
Unit II: Dictionaries and Query Processing = 06 Hours

Components of Index, Index Life Cycle, Static Inverted Index, Dictionaries-Types (Sort Based, Hash Based, Interleaving &
Posting Lists), Index Construction (In memory, Sort Based, Merge Based, Disk Based Index Construction), Dynamic Indexing,
Query Processing for Ranked Retrieval, Document at a Time Query Processing, Term at a Time Query Processing, Pre-
computing Score Contributions, Impact Ordering, Query Optimization.

Case Studies: Matching of the searched statement with the database which is already stored.
Unit I: Probabilistic Retrieval and Language Modelling Related Methods 06 Hours

Probabilistic Retrieval: Review of Basic Probability Theory; The Probability Ranking Principle: The 1/0 loss case, the PRP
with retrieval costs; The Binary Independence Model; Term Frequency; An appraisal and some extensions: An appraisal of
probabilistic models, tree-structured dependencies between terms, Okapi BM25: a non-binary model, Bayesian network
approaches to IR, Relevance Feedback, Field Weights: BM25F.

Language Models for Information Retrieval: generating queries from documents; Language Models: finite automata
and language models; Types of language models; Multinomial distributions over words; Ranking with Language Models;
Divergence from Randomness, Passage Retrieval, and Ranking.

Case Studies: A Comparative Study of Probabilistic and Language Models for Information Retrieval.
Unit IV: Text Classification and Text Clustering 06 Hours

Text Classification: Introduction to Text Classification, Naiyes Bayes Model, K Nearest neighbor, spam filtering, Support
Vector Machine Classifier, Vector Space classification using hyperplanes, kernel function.

Text Clustering: Clustering vs Classification, partitioning methods. Clustering Algorithms: k-means clustering,
Agglomerative hierarchical clustering, Expectation Maximization, Mixture of Gaussians Model.

Case Studies: Improving Document Organization and Retrieval in a Digital Library.
Unit V: Web Retrieval and Web Crawling 06 Hours
Parallel Information Retrieval: Parallel query processing, Mapreduce.

Web Retrieval: Search Engine Architectures, Cluster based Architecture, Distributed Architectures, Search Engine Ranking.
Link based Ranking, Page Ranking Algorithm, Simple Ranking Functions and Evaluations.

Web Crawler: Web Crawler structure, Web crawler libraries, Python Scrapy, Beautiful Soup, Applications.

Case Studies: Study of Google Map/ Geogusser information retrieval.

06 Hours

Multimedia Information Retrieval: Spoken Language Audio Retrieval, Non-Speech Audio Retrieval, Graph Retrieval,
Imagery Retrieval, Video Retrieval.

Recommender System: Collaborative Filtering, Content Based Recommendation, Knowledge Based Recommendation

Information Extraction and Integration: Extracting Data from Text. Semantic Web, Collecting and Integrating Specialized
Information on the web.

Case Studies: Demonstrate Collaborative filtering using any datasets to recommend items to users.

Although many IR models might be described as
probabilistic, the term is usually associated with the
methods arising from the work of Stephen Robertson,
Karen Sp'arck Jones, and their colleagues at the City
University London, at the University of Cambridge, and
at Microsoft Research, Cambridge, beginning in the
early 1970s.

Over that period they developed the probabilistic
retrieval model through a series of innovations that
have led to its current status as one of the most
successful IR models. The model has been adopted by
other research groups who have incorporated its
retrieval formulae into their experimental IR systems
and have often proposed and evaluated extensions of
their own. The organization of this chapter closely
follows the historical development of the model,
highlighting each significant extension and innovation
in turn, A substantial portion of the chapter builds
toward Equation, the BM25 formula, which currently
represents one of the best performing (and best-
known) retrieval formulae within the field.

Although the BM25 formula may be used without
understanding the details of its development, we cover
this development in considerable detail not only for its
own sake but also as a worked example of the
reasoning processes underlying much of IR theory. The

3.1.1 Review of Basic Probability Theory

The probabilistic model takes as its starting point the
Probability Ranking Principle: If an IR system's response
to each query is a ranking of the documents in the
collection in order of decreasing probability of
relevance, the overall effectiveness of the system to its
user will be maximized.

Probabilistic retrieval is an information retrieval
approach that uses probabilistic models to rank and
retrieve documents based on their relevance to a user's
query. Before delving into probabilistic retrieval, let us
review some basic concepts of probability theory that
are relevant to this approach:

> Sample Space (Q): The sample space represents
the set of all possible outcomes of an experiment
or event. For example, when flipping a fair coin, the
sample space is {Heads, Tails}.

> Event (E): An event is a subset of the sample
space, representing a _ specific outcome or
combination of outcomes. For instance, getting
“Heads” when flipping a coin is an event denoted
by E = {Heads}.

> Probability (P): The probability of an event E,
denoted by P(E), is a numerical value between 0
and 1 that indicates the likelihood of that event
occurring. A probability of 0 means the event is
impossible, while a probability of 1 means the
event is certain.

613

Frequency

+ We list a number of simple document features used by
basic ranking methods to compare documents and
determine an appropriate ranking. These features
include term frequency, term proximity, and document
length, Thus far, our development of the probabilistic
mode! has considered only term presence, essentially a
weakened form of term frequency. In order to extend
the model to fully accommodate term frequency, We
st return to Eq. (36) and reconsider our
representation of documents by the random variable D.

We refined our definition of the random variable D into
2 vector of binary random variables D = Dy, Dz, , where

random vat

can retrace our steps with a revised definition for D as
2 vector of random variables D = Fy, F:,, where each F,
represents the within-document term fre

here f, represents the number of times t appears in the

estimate values for p(F, = f,| 1) and piF, = {| 7), the
term frequency probabilities given relevance ond nom.
relevance. In order to estimate these values, we must sn
turn consider the relationship between term frequency
and relevance.

when writing a document about a particular topic, an

author will generally choose terms related to that topic.

‘As a result, when a term is associated with a particular
topic, we might expect that term to appear more
frequently in a document about the topic than in a
document not about the topic. On the other hand that
term will still occasionally appear in documents not
related to the topic

Bookstein and Swanson’s attempt to capture this,
relationship between topics and terms with a notion
that Robertson et al and Robertson and Walker call
eliteness. A document is said to be elite in term t when
it is somehow “about” the topic associated with the
term, By turning this relationship around it may thus be
possible for us to infer a probability of eliteness from
term frequency the greater the term frequency, the
more likely that the term is elite in the document. Thus,
the relationship between relevance and term frequency
flows through this notion of eliteness. Roughly,
documents about a topic are more likely to be elite in
the terms related to that topic and therefore these
terms are more likely to appear in these documents

For each term we define a binary random variable Et
corresponding to each Ft and representing eliteness of
the term t. & = 1 indicates that 2 document is elite in t
and & = 0 indicates that the document is not efite in t.

1 and @ as shorthand for = 0. We omit the

[5.3 AN APPRAISAL AND SOME EXTENSIONS |
3.3.1 An Appraisal of Probabilistic Models

Probabilistic models play a crucial role in Information
Retrieval GR) systems for renking and retr
relevant documents oased on the'r | e!/no0d of Deng

relevant to a user's query. Here is an appraisal of
probabilistic models used in IR

Advantages

+ Probabilistic Framework: Prosebilistic models prov
‘2 sound theoretical foundation for ranking documents
based on probabilities, making it easier to interpret and
understand the ranking results.

© Relevance Ranking: Probabilistic models directly
estimate the probability of relevance for documents,
enabling more accurate ranking compared to
traditional boolean retrieval models

for

Inverse

+ Term Weights: These models allow the
incorporation of term weights, such es
Document Frequency (IDF) and Term Frequency (TF)
which help capture the importance of terms in
documents and queries.

© Query Expansion: Probabilistic models can be
extended to support query expansion techniques.
improving retrieval effectiveness by including

synonymous terms.

probabil

+ Effectiveness for Long Querie
models, longer queries tend to perform better than in
as they allow term

for riche

boolean models,

weighting and relevance estimation.

+ Complexity: models

Parameter Tuning: The effectiveness of probabilistic

models often depends on the proper tuning of

parameters, which can be challenging,

© Query Ambiguity: Probab istic models may struggle
to handle ambiguous queries where the user's intent is

ar, leading to less accurate results,

* Vocabulary Mismatch: These models might suffer

from the vocabulary mismatch problem, where relevant

documents may not contain all query terms due to

differences in phrasing or language.

+ Data Sparsity: In cases of sparse data, especially for
rare terms. probabilistic models might struggle to
accurately estimate term probabilities.

1. Common Probabilistic Models in IR

+ Binary Independence Model (BIM): Assumes that
terms are conditionally independent given relevance. It
is simple end computationally efficient but may suffer
from the independence assumption

+ Okapi BM25 Model: & widely used probabilistic mode!

of BIM by

that addresses the shortcomings
incorporating term frequency saturation and document
length normalization. It often outperforms other
models in IR tasks.

Language Models (LM): Treat documents and queries
as language models and estimate the likelihood of
generating the query from the document language
model. LM-based models can be effective but require
smoothing techniques to handle zero probabilities.

Divergence from Randomness (DFR): Uses the
concept of divergence from randomness to estimate

relevance. DFR models offer good retrieval
performance and fleriblity in term weighting,
Applicability

Probabilistic models are well-suited for ad hoc retrieval
tasks, where users submit one-time queries and expect

relevant documents from

tion. They are

effective in web search engines, document databases

and information retrieval systerns with large-scale

document collections

43.3.2 Tree-Structured Depende

Terms:

Tree-structured dependencies between terms refer to 3

hierarchical relationship among terms in an Information

fal (IR) system, In this context, terms can represent

ncies Between

Retrieve
query keywords, document content, oF any textual

information that needs to be organized based on their

relationships. The tree structure implies that the terms are

organized in o parent-child relationship, forming a tree-lke
hierarchy.

+ Hierarchical Concept Representati
application of tree-structured dependencies is in
representing hierarchical concepts. In IR, terms can be
grouped into higher-level concepts, forming a tree
where parent terms represent broader concepts, and
child terms represent more specific sub-concepts. This
hierarchical representation allows for more efficient
query processing and can improve the accuracy of
document ranking.

One common

Taxonomy or Ontology Construction: Tree-
structured dependencies are often used to construct
taxonomies or ontologies, which are formal
representations of the relationships between terms in a
specific domain. Taxonomies organize terms
hierarchically based on their semantic relationships,
while ontologies also capture additional properties and
relationships, allowing for more sophisticated
knowledge representation.

Query Expansion: In query expansion, tree-structured
dependencies can be leveraged to include related
terms in the user's query. By traversing the tree and
adding child terms to the original query, the system
can broaden the search scope and retrieve more
relevant documents.

and Identification

Synonym Hyponym By

organizing terms in a tree structure, the system can
identify synonymous terms (different terms with similar
meanings) and hyponyms (more specific terms related
10 @ broader term). This information can be used for
query espansion, disarnbiguation, and better document

ron

into knowledge graphs, which represent knowledge in
a structured and interconnected manner

Okapi BM2S is @ popular probabilistic retrieval model
used in Information Retrieval (IR) systems to rank
documents based on their relevance to a user's query.
It is an improved version of the Binary Independence
Model (BIM) and is named after the British Museum in
London, where the model was developed in the 1980s
BM25 is widely used in modern search engines and
document retrieval systems due to its effectiveness and
simplicity. Here is an overview of the Okapi BM25

model:
Term Frequency and Inverse Document Frequency:
BM25 calculates the relevance score of a document for
a given query by considering two important factors:
(i) Term Frequency (TF): The number of times 2
query term appears in the document. Documents
with higher term frequencies are considered more
relevant to the query.
(ii) Inverse Document Frequency (IDF): A measure of
how rare a term is in the entire document
collection, Rare terms receive higher IDF scores
indicating their importance in distinguishing

3.4.2 Bayesian Network Approaches to IR

«Turtle and Croft introduced into information retrieval
the use of Bayesian networks a form of probabilistic
graphical model We skip the details because fully
Introducing the forma! s™ of Bayesian networks would
require much too much space, but conceptually,
Bayesian networks use directed graphs to show
probabilistic dependencies between variables, and have
led to the develoo™ent of sophisticated algorithms for

propagating in so as to allow learning and
nference win arpiary knowledge within arbitrary
directed acycic graons Turtle and Croft used a
sop 3 network to better model the complex

dependencies between a document and a user's

information need

+ The model decomposes into two parts: a document
collection network and a query network. The document

fon network is large, but can be precomputed it

ments to terms to concepts. The
5 are a thesaurus-based expansion of the terms
appearing in the document. The query network is
wll but a new network needs to be built
time 2 query comes in, and then attached to the
document network

relatively

The query network maps from
to query sub-expressions (built using
probabilistic or “noisy” versions of AND and OR
user's information need

operators), to the

The result is a flexible probabilistic network which can
generalize various simpler Boolean and probabilistic
models. Indeed. this is the primary case of a statistical
ranked retrieval model that naturally supports
structured query operators. The system allowed
efficient large-scale retrieval, and was the basis of the
In Query text retrieval system, built at the University of
Massachusetts. This system performed very well in
TREC evaluations and for a time was sold commercially
On the other hand, the model still used various
approximations and independence assumptions to

make parameter estimation and computation possible.

There has not been much follow-on work along these

lines, but we would note that this model was actually

built very early on in the modern era of using Bayesian

Networks, and there have been many subsequent

PROBABILISTIC RETRIEVAL AND LANGUAGE

«Bayesian network approaches have been applied 19
Information Retrieval (R) as 2 way to Model ang
represent the relationships between query terms
documents, and relevance. Bayesian networks arg
probabilistic graphical models that use Directed Acycic
Graphs (DAGs) to model dependencies between
random variables. In thé context of IR, Bayesian
networks provide a structured and probabilistic
framework to estimate the relevance of documents to g
given query. Here is an overview of how Bayesian
network approaches are applied to IR:
> Graphical Representation: In 2 Bayesian network

for IR, nodes in the graph represent random
variables, and directed edges indicate conditiona|
dependencies. The nodes in the graph typically
include query terms, document content, relevance
scores, and other contextual information.
> Probability Estimation: Bayesian networks use
probability distributions to model the relationships
between the nodes. Conditional probability tables
(CPT) specify the probabilities for each node given
its parent nodes in the graph. These probabilities
are often estimated from training data using
statistical techniques like maximum likelihood
estimation
> Query-Document Relevance _Estimation:
Bayesian networks can be used to estimate the
relevance of a document to a given query. The
query terms are treated as observed evidence, and
the relevance score of the document is treated as
the target variable to be estimated.

3 Learning from Data: Bayesian networks can be
learned from data, using techniques like the
Expectation-Maximization (EM)
Bayesian parameter estimation. Learning the
network structure and CPTs from data can adapt
the model to specific domains and improve its
performance.

algorithm or

> Query Expansion: Bayesian networks can aid in
query expansion by capturing dependencies
between query terms. By traversing the network
related terms can be identified and added to the
original query, leading to more comprehensive and
effective retrieval.
Relevance Feedback: Bayesian networks can be
used to incorporate relevance feedback from usets

to improve the retrieval results. User feedback 0”

> Uncertainty Handling: One of the key advantages
of Bayesian networks is their ability to handle
uncertainty naturally. By explicitly modeling
probabilities, Bayesian networks can account for
uncertainty in relevance estimation and document
ranking
Overall, Bayesian network approaches to IR offer a
powerful and flexible framework for modeling
dependencies between query terms and documents,
capturing semantic relationships, handling uncertainty,
and incorporating user feedback. They have been
successfully applied in various IR tasks, including ad
hoc retrieval, query expansion, and relevance feedback
and continue to be an active area of research in the
field of Information Retrieval.

fon the field in which a term occurs. For example, we

in the document and scale the term frequency
accordingly

d. We then apply field-specific document length
normalization similar to the BM25 normalization but
applied at the field level, reflecting the differences in
‘average length between different field types. For
‘example, the title of a document is usually very short
relative to its body

+ Ths very simple formula pays the same oe in relation
to the language modeling approach Given a document
d and 2 query g we score d for ranking purposes by
estimating the probability 21g | d)

(3.26)

In order to estimate this probability, the document is
often considered to provide a model for generating the
query q. Imagine 2 user formulating a query to retrieve
relevant documents. She might attempt to think of
some terms that should appear often in these relevant
documents but rarely in nonrelevant documents. In
making this attempt the user forms an image in her
mind of what 2 relevant document looks like what
terms might distinguish it from other documents in the
collection. She then selects a few of these terms and
enters them into 2 search engine. These terms become
the query q
To score d we assume it matches the image in the
user's mind and estimate the probability that the user
would select q to retrieve it It is because of this
assumed link between the user's information need the
image in her mind and the document d that we can
view d as providing a generative model for q. Some IR
researchers justify £q. (3.26) more directly through a
different reasoning process. They simply state that we
are interested in p(Q D), the joint distribution of
queries and documents. The issue of relevance is
ignored. Documents are ranked by

[3.8 TYPES OF LANGUAGE MODELS

3.8.1 Multinomial Distributions Over Words

Multinomial distributions over
words in a given context. typically in the context of Natural
Language Processing (NLP) and Information Retrieval (R).
The multinomial distribution is 2 generalization of the
binomial distribution, which models the number of
successes in a fixed number of independent Bernoulli trials.
In the context of words, the multinomial distribution
represents the probabilities of observing different words
from a predefined vocabulary in a document or a sequence

of words

+ Multinomial Distribution: The multinomial

distribution models the probabilities of different
outcomes in a categorical experiment. In the context of
words, the outcomes are the occurrences of individual
words from a fixed vocabulary in 2 given context (e.g.
2 document 2 sentence, or a bag of words).

+ Word Frequencies: In NLP and IR, 2 common way to
represent text is through the bag-of-words model,
where 2 document is represented as a vector of word
frequencies. Zach element of the vector corresponds to
the frequency of a specific word from the vocabulary in
the document. The multinomial distribution can be

used to model the probabilities of observing each word
frequency in the document

* Probability Estimation: To estimate the multinomial
probabilities, one typically uses Maximum Likelihood
Estimation (MLE) based on a training corpus. Given a
corpus of documents, the probability of observing each
word's frequency in a document can be estimated as
the relative frequency of that word in the corpus.

Language Models: Multinomial distributions over
words are commonly used in language modeling.
Language models aim to estimate the probability of a
sequence of words in a language. A simple form of a
language model is the n-gram model, where the
probability of a word is estimated based on the
‘occurrence of the previous (n-1) words. The n-gram
probabilities can be modeled using multinomial
distributions over words.

Information Retrieval: In IR, multinomial distributions
can be used to model the occurrence of query terms in
documents and estimate the relevance of documents
PAVIA

words are a type of
probability distribution used to model the occurrence of

Document Classification: Multinomial distribyy,
can be used in text classification tasks, where the
is to classify documents into predefined catego,
based on word frequencies. es
Language Generation: Multinomial distribution ,
used in text generation tasks, such a5 lang,,"
generation, machine translation, and 4a
summarization, 10 generate coherent and yo!

ions

sentences.
+ Smoothing Techniques: In practice, some Words in
corpus may have zero frequencies for certain conten,
leading to zer0 probabilities in the multinoms

distribution. To address this issue, smooth
techniques like Laplace (edd-one) smoothing
Dirichlet smoothing are used to avoid zen,

probabilities and improve the robustness of languag
models.

Kullback-Leibler Divergence

* An alternative theoretical framework for understanding
and working with the language modeling approach is
provided by a concept known as Kullback-Leibler
divergence. KL divergence, also known as relative
entropy, is a method for comparing two probability

processes, just as we did for the document language
models. We then apply KL divergence to rank
documents by computing the divergence between the
query language model and the document language
model:

Divergence from Randomness

The Divergence From Randomness (DFR) approach to
information retrieval explicitly assumes random
process for the distribution of terms in documents, and
then ranks documents by considering the probability
that the actual term distribution found in a document
would occur by chance. Similar considerations appear
implicitly in the language modeling approach through
its incorporation of a collection language model into
the smoothing process.

3,9 A MODEL OF RANDOMNESS

Suppose we randomly distribute terms into documents
If we have h occurrences of term t distributed acrose N

How many different ways can , occurrences be
distributed across N documents, assuming that the
documents are indistinguishable? For example, four
occurrences can be distributed across three documents
in four different ways:
1. Alloccurrences into one document,
2, Three occurrences into one document and one into
another,
3, Two occurrences in one document and one in each
of the other documents,
4, Two occurrences in two documents. In other
words, how many different arrangements of terms
will satisfy Eq. (3.47)?
To answer this question, Amati and van Rijsbergen
recognize that this problem is identical to the problem
addressed by Bose-Einstein statistics, which computes
the number of ways indistinguishable particles may be
assigned to energy states in thermal equilibrium. The
solution may be expressed as a binomial coefficient:
(* + b-2) (N+ 4-2)!
& (=D!
Alternatively, the problem may be viewed as equivalent
to the problem of determining the number of ways in
which m balls can be distributed across
indistinguishable bins, a version of the occupancy
problem from combinatorics. In order to compute an
estimate of PL for term t, we assume that a given
document d is found to contain fys occurrences of t. A
random distribution of the remaining k ~ fea
‘occurrences into the remaining documents must satisfy

3.9.1 Passage Retrieval and Ranking

+ Most of the ranking methods we examine in this book
rank documents. Depending on the application
environment, these documents may correspond to
Web pages, news articles, e-mail messages. or a
combination of these and other document types. In
some environments it may be appropriate to rank
elements within documents, such as the pages in a
book or the sections in a news article. In other
circumstances arbitrary passage retrieval may be
desirable so that any fragment of text within a
document may be returned as a ranked result.

One such circumstance is generation of snippets to

simple. summary

Question answering is another application in which
arbitrary passage retrieval may be valuable Given a
Question such as “What is the population of India?”, 2
question answering system attempts to provide an
12 billion’) rather than a document
that may contain the answer. Passage retrieval is often
an early sted in question answering. Keywords are
extracted from the question and formed into a query
for processing by an IR system ( “population’,“india’)
Passages containing these terms are then retrieved
from the corpus and analyzed to extract and validate
possible answers. An interval containing the query
terms in close proximity ("..population of India, ..") is
likely to be part of a slightly longer but still short
passage containing the answer ("..The population of
India is...) The interval represents a “hot spot” within
the collection near which the answer may be found

exact answer

We now extend the notion of a cover to subsets of the
query terms. We define an m-cover for m sn terms as
an interval [u, v] in the collection that contains an
occurrence of at least m distinct terms in the query and
does not contain a smaller interval that also contains m
distinct terms. For simplicity we assume that terms are
not repeated in q because the passage ranking method
in this section does not take repeated query terms into
account. For exemple, the set of 2-covers for the query
vector "you", "quarrel", “sir” over the collection in Table
2.2 (page 50) consists of the intervals (2, 3], (3, 4) (4, 5]
(5, 6}, (8, 10}, (20, 12), (12, 16), (24, 28). Note that the
interval (12, 24] is not included in the set because it
contains [12, 16]. Note also that (24, 28) is included
even though it cuts across the last three documents. In

most applications m-covers such as this one would be

filtered from the set before use. However, for simplicity

we define m-covers without consideration for

document boundaries and other structures and apply a
post-processing pass as appropriate.
The notion of an m-cover may be used to support
snippet generation and arbitrary passage retrieval for

and details. More generally, for applications such as
snippet generation and question answering, we must
select intervals by trading off length against the
‘combination of query terms they contain

has a history, which dates back to the
beginning of the 1960s, But the incredible increase in
available online documents in the last two decades.
due to the expanding internet, has intensified and
fenewed the interest. in automated document
classification and data mining. In the beginning text
classification focussed on heuristic methods, ie. solving
the task by applying a set of rules based on expert
knowledge. This approach proved to be highly
inefficient. so nowadays the focus has turned to fully
‘automatic learning and clustering methods.
The task of text classification consists in assigning 2
document to one or more categories, based on the
semantic content of the document. Document (or text)
classification runs in two modes:
(The training phase
(i) Prediction (or classification) phase
The training phase can be divided into three kinds:
(supervised document classification is performed by
an external mechanism, usually human feedback,
which provides the necessary information for the
correct classification of documents,

(ii) Semi-supervised document classification, a mixture
between supervised and —_ unsupervised
classification: some documents or parts of
documents are labelled by external assistance,

(ii) Unsupervised document classification is entirely
executed without reference to external information,

tert classifier and it is the focus of research in text

Here's 2 step-by-step overview of how the Naive gp,
model works for text classification:

Step 1: Data Preprocessing: As with any text classifate,
task, the first step is to preprocess the text data 1,
includes tokenization, converting text to lovers:
removing punctuation, and filtering out stop words

Step 2: Feature Extraction: To represent the tert dy
numerically, the model typically uses the Bag-of-Wor
(GoW) representation. BOW is a simple and effective wsjc
representing text data, where each document ;
represented as a vector of word frequencies Te
vocabulary is constructed from the entire corpus of tary
data

Step 3: Calculating Class Priors: The Naive Bayes mote
calculates the prior probabilities of each class label (te
probability that a document belongs to a specific ces
This is done by counting the occurrences of each clas itt
in the training data and dividing it by the total number?
training documents,

Step 4: Calculating Likelihoods: For each class, the mo!
calculates the likelihood of each word occuring *
documents of that class. This is done by counting t
occurtences of each word in documents of that cass?

viding it by the total number of words in all docu
of that class

3. When to Use Naive Bayes?
+ A Naive Bayesian classifier performs worse than @
complex classifier due to the strict assumptions it
makes about the data, The classifier, however, has
some advantages:
> Training and predicting the model is done at @
high speed.
Probabilistic predictions can be created purely
based on the data.

y

> They are usually pretty easy to interpret.

y

Their parameters are usually not tunable.
1. High-speed training and prediction
2. Probability prediction based on data
3. Easy to interpret,

4. Non-tunable parameters

* An initial baseline classifier based on a Naive Bayesian
Classifier offers these advantages. In case it performs
well, you will have a classifier for your problem that is
intuitive and very fast to interpret.

+ With some basic knowledge of how well they should
Perform, you can explore more sophisticated models if
it does not perform welt intially

4.1.3 K-Nearest Neighbor

+ K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN) is a simple and intuitive
algorithm used for text classification and other
machine learning tasks. It is a non-parametric and lary
learning algorithm, meaning it doesn't make explicit
assumptions about the underlying data distribution
and doesn't build an explicit model during the training
phase. Instead, it memorizes the training data and class
labels to make predictions on new, unseen data.

‘+The basic idea behind KNN is to classify a new data

point based on the majority class of its "k" nearest
neighbors in the feature space. For text classification,

{TION RETRIEVAL

Ht
q

resent h numerical

‘cow or Term Frequency-Inversa — 839 of. Words

(rri0F) vectors ument Frequency

jre's an OVETVIEW Of how Ay

daassification:

py Date Preprocessing: pg
classification approaches,
preprocess the text data,

IN works for tert

ten
step is to
ling tokenization,
‘uation removal, and

with,
the first

Feature Extraction: Convent the prepoc
data into numerical feature vectors such sate
TP-IOF. 8s BOW or

Distance Metric: Choose an approprite dita
re the Simla or ent
vectors. Common di
for text data include Euclidean dist
distance, oF Cosine similarity

‘stance metrics
tance, Manhattan

Choose Select the value of “k* which
represents the number of nearest neighbore wn
consider when making predictions The valve of
is a hyperparameter that can be chos,
cross-validation or other tuning metho

en through
ds

For each new document to classify,
nearest neighbors in the training set
based on the chosen distance metic these
neighbors are the training documents with the
closest feature vectors to the new document

ication:

> Majority Voting: Determine the majority class
among the "k" nearest neighbors. The new
document is assigned the class label that appears
most frequently among its neighbors. In case of a
tie, the class label of the nearest neighbor with the
smallest distance can be used,

KNIN is an instance-based algorithm, which means it
doesn't have a traditional training phase. The entire
training dataset is kept in memory, making the
prediction phase potentially slower for large datasets.
However, it can be computationally efficent for smaller
datasets.

Let's first understand the term neighbors here. The
closeness / proximity amongst samples of data
determines their neighborhood, There are several ways

depending on the problem to solved. tMost farniliar and

Popular is straight-line distance (Euclidean D

ance)
Generally, neighbors share similar charac
behavior that’s why they can be treated as
to the same group

This is the main idea of this simple supervised learning
Classification algorithm,

Now, for the K in KNN algorithm that is we consider
the K-Nearest Neighbors of the unknown data we want
to classify and assign it the group appearing majorly in
those K neighbors. For K=1, the unknown/unlabeled
data will be assigned the class of its closest neighbor

We want to select a value of K that is reasonable and
not something too big (it will predict the class having
‘majority among all data samples) or something too
small

Let's see how this works on this example dataset of music
fans,

Now, we have a person named Gary who is @ 23 year
male and we want to predict which band will he like
more, Here's how we can use the KNN algorithm,

Firstly we'll have to translate gender to some numbers

neighbors.

We do this by translating male->0 and female->1

For each data entry distance is caleulated from Gary and
distance for ith data is given 2s,

\begintequation) \sqrt{(age_i- oge_(Gary))"2 +
(gender_i - gender_{Gary))*2} \end{equation}

+ Let's say we have our text data represented in feature

Here we are pre-processing on text and generating feature
vectors of token counts and then transform into tridf
representation.

Consider a document containing 100 words wherein the
word ‘car’ appears 7 times.

The term frequency (tf) for phone is then (7 / 100) = 0.07.
Now, assume we have 1 million documents and the word
car appears in one thousand of these, Then, the inverse
document frequency (Le, IDF) is calculated as log(10,00,000
1100) = 4. Thus, the T-IDF weight is the product of these
quantities: 0.07 * 4 = 0.28,

i)

__TEXT CLASSIFICATION AND TUXT CLUSTERING

Step

Data pre-processing

This is an important step in any data mining process. This
basically involves transforming raw data into an
understandable format for NLP models. Real-world data is
often incomplete, inconsistent, and/or lacking in certain
behaviors or trends, and is likely to contain many errors
Data pre-processing is a proven method of resolving such
issues. This will help in getting better results through the
classification algorithms.

+ Below, I have explained the two techniques that are
also performed besides other easy to understand steps
in data pre-processing:
> Tokenization: This is a process of breaking @
stream of text up into words, phrases, symbols. or
other meaningful elements called tokens. The list
‘of tokens becomes input for further processing
NLTK Library has word_tokenize and sent_toxenize
to easily break a stream of text into a list of words
or sentences, respectively.

> Word Stemming/Lemmatization: The aim of
both processes is the same, reducing the
inflectional forms of each word into a common
base or root. Lemmatization is closely related to
stemming. The difference is that a stemmer

Step

Word Vectorization

I is a general process of turning a collection of text
documents into numerical feature vectors. Their are many
methods to convert text data to vectors which the mode!
2 Understand but by fer the most popular method is
called TE-IOF. This is an acronym than stands for Term
Frequency — Inverse Document” Frequency which are the
components of the resulting scores assigned to each word
+ Term Frequency: This summarizes how often a given
word appears within a document.

Inverse Document Frequency: This down  sceles
‘words that appear a lot across documents

Without going into the math, TF-IDF are word frequency
scores that try to highlight words that are more interesting,
9. frequent in a document but not across documents.

The following syntax can be used to first fit the TG-IDF
‘model on the whole corpus. This will help TF-IOF build a
vocabulary of words which it has learned from the corpus
data and it will assign 2 unique integer number to each of
these words. Their will be maximum of $000 unique words
features as we have set parameter max features = 5000.
Finally we will transform Train X and Test_X to vectorized
Train_X_Tfidf and Test_XTfidf, These will now contain for
each row a list of unique integer number and its associated
importance as calculated by TF-IDF

4.2.1 Clustering vs Classification

Text clustering and text classification are both technigg.
eed in Natural Language Processing (NLP) to analyze 4,
rganize text data. However, they serve different purpos
and have distinct approaches:

1. Text Clustering:

Clustering is an unsupervised learning technique tha,
groups similar data points together based on they
features without any predefined labels or categories,

In the context of text data, text clustering involves

organizing 2 collection of documents into clusters

where documents within the same cluster are more

similar to each other than to documents in other

clusters

+ The similarity between documents is typically measure
Using distance metrics such as cosine similarity,
Euclidean distance, or Jaccard similarity, depending on
the representation of the text data (e.g., Bag-of- Words,
TF-IDF, word embeddings).

+ Common clustering algorithms for text data include k-

means, hierarchical clustering, and  densit

Here are Some Well-known Partitioning Methods for

Text Clusterin

1. K-Means Clustering:

K-Means is one of the most popular partition
algorithms for text clustering

1 requires specifying the number of cl

The algorithm initializes K centroids randomly, and
then iteratively assigns each data point to the nearest
centroid and updates the centroids’ positions based on
the assigned points

The process continues until the centroids stabilize or 2
‘maximum number of iterations is reached

K-Means aims to minimize the sum of squared
distances between data points and their assigned

K-Mean (A Centroid Based Technique): The « means
algorithm takes the input parameter K from the user
and partitions the dataset containing N objects into
clusters so that resulting similarity among the date
objects inside the group (intracluster) is high but the
similarity of data objects with the data objects from
outside the cluster is low (intercluster). The similarity of
the cluster is determined with respect to the mean
value of the cluster. It is a type of square error

clustering methods like DBSCAN.
Text clustering is useful for tasks such as document
organization, topic modeling, and identifying hidden
structures in large text corpora.

Text clustering is the process of grouping similar text
documents together based on their content. One category
of clustering algorithms commonly used for text data is
pattitioning methods. Partitioning methods aim to divide
the data into a fixed number of clusters, where each data

dataset are chosen in which each of the objects
represents» cluster mean (cen) For the rest ofthe
date object, they ae assigned to the neoest cluster

Hierarchical Clustering

Step 1: Data Re}
into 2 numerical
TF-IDF or Bag-of-Words

e

stance Matrix: Compute the painvise distancy
5 using 3 distance metric such 3,

Step 2:
between all data point
Eucideen distance or cosine similarity. This creates
distance mateix.

Step 3: Initial Clusters: Assign each data Point t0 its og,
individual cluster.

Step 4: Merge Closest Clusters: Find the two closey
dusters based on the distance matrix. The distance
between clusters can be measured using different linkage
methods like single linkage, complete linkage, or average
linkage. Merge these two closest clusters into a new cluster
Step 5: Update Distance Matrix: Recalculate the distances
between the newly formed cluster and all other clusters in

the distance matrix.

Step 6: Repeat Merging and Updating: Repeat steps 4
and § iteratively until all data points belong to a single
cluster or until a specified number of clusters is reached

Step 7: Dendrogram Visualization: Plot the dendrogram
which visualizes the hierarchical clustering process and

shows how clusters are merged at each step.

4.3.3 Expecta

Text clustering

using
Typically involves the use of Gaussian Mixt
(GMMs). GMMs are a probabilistic model that assu

pectation-Max

data points in each cluster are generated from 2
several Gaussian distributions. EM is used to estimate
Parameters of the Gaussian components and to ass
Points to their most likely clusters

Theoretical Steps for Text Clustering using EMt with GMM
Step 1: Data Representation: Convert the raw text dats
into a numerical representation using techniques like TF
IDF or 8ag-of-Words,

Step 2: Initialize GMM Parameters: Choose the number
of clusters (K) that you want to create

Initialize the parameters of K Gaussian components. These
Parameters include the mean, covariance matrix, and
‘mixing coefficient (prior probability) for each component
Step 3: Expectation Step (E-Step): Calculate the
likelihood of each data point belonging to each Gaussian
component using the current GMM parameters

Use Bayes’ theorem to calculate the posterior probabilities
(responsibilities) of each data point for each Gaussian
component

Step 4: Maximization Step (M-step): Update the GMM
parameters (mean, covariance, mixing coefficient) based on
the responsibilities calculated in the E-step.

Re-estimate the mean and covariance of each Gaussian
‘component using the weighted data points assigned to it
Update the mixing coefficient based on the average
responsibility of each component.

===== PDF page_26
INFORMATION RETRIEVAL 82 sey predien(Xto0rrey0) Ny
" jyster_lobelé =

Step 5: Iterative Update: Repeat the E-step and M-steP lust

‘maximum number of iterations is reached gp Print the cle

StgP 6 Convergence Criterion: the M algonthn YPEANY | fap 1 nrONGET Ss pere(cluster_labels x y

ops when the Change in the log-likelihood or the model | yeter I Noy

Parameters falls below a speciied tolerance threshold
indicating that the algorithm has reached a stable solution

For this example.

1.1 Parallel Query Processing
‘While nobody knows the exact sie of the GOI
part of the Web, tis estmated to te ey oes
billion pages. In August 2005, when Yahoo! last
disclosed the size of its index. it had reaches » tote!
size of 19.2 billion documents
(http://www ysearchblog com/archives/000172 htm!)
There are many ways in which paralieism can help 2
search engine process queries faster. The two most
popular approaches are index partitionng snd
replication. Suppose we have a total of n index servers
Following the standard terminology, we refer to these
servers as nodes. By creating n replicas of the index
and assigning each replica to a separate nade, we can
realize an n-fold increase of the search engine's service
rate (its theoretical throughput) without affecting the
time required to process @ single query. This type of
parallelism is referred to as inter-query parallelism,
because multiple queries can be processed in parallel

Note that the pipelined query processing architecture
outlined above does not use intra-query parallelism, as
each query at 2 given point in time is processed by only a
single node Therefore, although it can help increase the
system's theoretical throughput, term partitioning, in the
simple form described here, does not necessarily decrease
the search engine's response time. To some extent this
limitation can be addressed by having the receptionist send
prefetch instructions to the nodes v(t), ..., v(t) at the same
time it fonwerds the query to v(ty). This way, when a node
Vit) receives the accumulator set, some of t.'s postings may
already have been loaded into memory and the query can
be processed faster. Despite its potential performance
advantage over the document-partitioned approach, at
least for on-disk indices, term partitioning has several
Shortcomings that make it difficult to use the method in
practice:

> Scalability: As the collection becomes bigger, so
do the individual postings lists. For a corpus
composed of a billion documents, the postings list
for a frequent term, such as “computer” or
“internet”, can easily occupy several hundred
megabytes. Processing a query that contains one
or more of these frequent terms will require at
least a few seconds, far more than what most users
are accustomed to. In order to solve this problem,
large postings lists need to be cut into smaller
chunks and divided among several nodes, with

for a query contal

each node taking care of 2 small part of

1 and all of them working in porate
this complicates the "gue
quite a bit

postings list
Unfortunately,
processing logic
Load Imbalance: Term partitioning suffers from ay
uneven load across the index nodes. The joay
corresponding to @ single term is a function of the
term's frequency in the collection as well as i
frequency in users’ queries. If @ term has a lon,
postings list and is also very popular in search
queries, then the corresponding index node may
experience 2 load that is much higher than the
‘average load in the system. To address thy
problem, postings lists that are responsible for 3
high fraction of the overall load should be
replicated and distributed across multiple nodes
This way, the computational load associated with a
given term can be shared among several machines,

albeit at the cost of increased storage
requirements
> Term-at-a-Time: Perhaps the most severe

limitation of the term-partitioned approach is its
inability to support efficient document-at-a-time
query processing, In order to realize document-at-
a-time scoring on top of a term-partitioned index
entire postings lists, as opposed to pruned
accumulator sets, would need to be sent across the
network. This is impractical, due to the size of the

The map phase may overlap with the shuffle phase.
and the shutfie phase may overlap with the reduce
Phase. However, the map phase may never overlap
with the reduce phase. The reason for this is that the
reduce function can only be called after all values for a

given key are available. Since. in general, it is

impossible to predict what keys the map workers will
emit, the reduce phase cannot commence before the
map phase is finished.

> Single Site Crawlers: These crawlets focus on
indexing documents from a single website or
domain. They are commonly used for site-specific
search engines.
> Topical or Focused Crawlers: Topical or focused
crawlers target specific topics or subjects,
concentrating their efforts on relevant websites

and documents within a particular vertical or

Conversion: Conversion is the process of transforming
documents from various formats, such as HTML XML
Word, POF, etc, into a consistent text plus metadata
format, which is typically in XML. The conversion
process aims to make the content easily accessible and
searchable by the search engine

===== PDF page_33
' sence

By employing feeds and conversion processes, search
engines can efficiently acquire documents from a variety of
sources and formats, ensuring that the content is made
accessible for indexing and search. Real-time feeds enable
the search engine to stay up-to-date with the latest
content, and conversion allows the system to present 2
Unified view of the content in a consistent manner.

2

Document Storage is a critical component of search engine
architectures, responsible for storing text, metadata, and
other related content for documents in 2 format that
enables fast access and retrieval. Efficient document
storage is essential to support various search engine
components, such as result list generation, and handle the
massive volume of documents encountered in large-scale
search engines.

Key Aspects of Document Storage

INFORMATION RETRIEVAL

> Document Format Conversion: Since documents
can eist in diverse formats, the search engine
Needs to convert them into a unified format ©
ensure uniform indexing. For example, web Page
may be in HTML format, while some documents
could be in proprietary formats like Word oF POF.
Conversion to XML standardizes the content for
indexing

Text Encoding Conversion: Different languages
use various text encodings, such as UTF-8, UTF-16.
10-8859, etc. Converting the text encoding t©
Unicode standard like UTF-8 ensures that the
search engine can process and display content in
different languages accurately.

Document Storage

Storing Text and Metadata: Document storage
involves the storage of both the main text content of
documents and associated metadata, The main text
content typically consists of the textual information
that users search for, such as the content of web pages,
articles, or documents. Metadata provides additional
information about the document, such as its type
(eg. web page, PDF, video), creation date, author,
language, and other relevant attributes. Storing both
text and metadata in a structured manner is crucial for
efficiently managing and retrieving documents during
the search process.

Storage of Other Related Content: In addition to text
and metadata, document storage may also include
related content, such as links and anchor text. For web
documents, links refer to hyperlinks present in 2

other web pages. a,

‘Stop Words Removal: Stop Words are common words
that occur frequently in most documents and
carry little meaningful information about
document's content. Examples of Stop Words include
'and,” “or,” “the,” “in,” "a." “is,
Words during text transformation can lead to 3 more
compact and efficient index

> Impact on Efficiency and Effectiveness:
Removing Stop Words reduces the size of the
index and speeds up the search process as there
are fewer terms to process. It can significantly
improve the efficiency of the search engine
Particularly for large-scale systems. However, there
might be a trade-off between efficiency and
effectiveness, as some Stop Words con carry
essential meaning in certain contexts
> Problem for Some Queries: For specific queries
that heavily rely on Stop Words, their removal can
negatively impact search results’ relevance For
example, queries like "the best restaurants in New
York" might suffer from relevant documents being
excluded if "the" and “in” are treated as Stop
Words.
Stemming: Stemming is a technique used to group
words derived from the same root or stem together. By
reducing words to their base form, stemming can
enhance recall by ensuring that variations of a word are
matched to the same root term during search.
Example: For the word “computers.” “computing,” and
“compute,” stemming would transform them all to the
common stem “comput” This way, @ search for
“computing” would also return documents containing
‘computers and “compute.
> Effectiveness and Limitations: Stemming is
Usually effective in improving recall and capturing
relevant documents containing variations of the
same term. However, it is not always accurate, and
there might be cases where stemming results in
irrelevant matches. For some languages with
complex word inflections, stemming might not be
as effective

> Benefits Vary for Different Languages: T°
effectiveness of stemming varies across different
languages. as some languages have more regula
word inflections, making stemming more reliable
In contrast. others have highly iregulst 2%
complex inflections, making accurate stemm"d
more challenging

(9) information Extraction

Information Extraction is a crucial process that involves
identifying specific classes of index terms or metadata
from the text to enhance the understanding and
representation of documents. Information extraction
techniques aim to extract structured and valuable
information from unstructured text. making it more
useful for various applications. Two key components of
information extraction are Named Entity Recognition
and Classification

« Named Entity Recognition (NER): Named Entity
Recognition is a subtask of information extraction that
focuses on identifying and classifying named entities
within the text. Named entities are specific words or
phrases that represent entities of interest, such as

oN
4 ose
gray Known TOK analysis aigg a3)
wwe sh elue PageRank ac" WEB RETRIEVAL AND WEB CRAWLING

People’s names, locations, organizations, dates, and
other named entities with real-world significance
Example: In the sentence “John works at Google 19
New York and was born on January Ast. 1990"
ould identify “John” as a person. “Google” 2: 27
Organization, “New York” as a location. and “January
1st 1990" as a date

NER is essential for various applications such as search
engines, question-answering systems, information
Fetvieval, and data mining, where extracting named
entities is crucial for understanding the context and
intent of the text

Classification: Classification is another critical aspect
©f information extraction, where documents are
assigned labels or categories based on their content. A
Classifier is a machine learning algorithm thet learns
from labeled examples to predict the appropriate class
label for new, unseen documents.

Example: In a news article classification task
documents may be assigned labels such as “politics.”
“sports,” “technology,” ete, based on their content and
topic

Classifiers can be used to assign class-related metadats
to documents, which can include information such 25
topics, reading levels, sentiment. genre. and more, The
specific use of classification depends on the
application's requirements and the types of metadata
needed for organizing and presenting the documents
effectively.
Information extraction, including NER and classificetion.
enhances the structure and semantic understanding of
Unstructured text, making it more accessible and valuable
for various applications. By identifying named entities and
assigning labels or categories to documents, information
extraction supports tasks like content organization,
filtering, recommendation systems, and more. A
fundamental part of natural language processing and text
analytics empowers a wide range of intelligent systems and
applications,

Statistics and Weighting.

» tnverted Index: An inverted index is a gy,

the documents in which they appear. It essentign,

2 Fast Query Processing: The format of the inverted

Large Document Collections: With billions of web
pages available on the internet, creating and managing
an index for such a massive collection is a daunting
task. Each web page contains numerous words, and
tracking the appearance of every term in every
document — requires substantial
resources.

computational

* Single Index on One Computer: Creating a single

index on a single computer for such an extensive
collection of documents is impractical. The
computational and storage requirements would be
‘overwhelming, leading to slow response times for
search queries.

Amn

involves coordinating multiple computers

work as a single system In the context of search engine
indexing, this means distributing the
collection

across multiple mach
them in parallel

MapReduce: Google's MapReduce framewor’
technology used to distribute
multiple machines. It enables efficient processing of
large datasets by breaking them into smaller “map”
tasks, processing them in parallel, and then combining
the results Creduce") to create the index

Sharding: Another technique used in distributed
systems is sharding, where the document collection is.
divided into smaller subsets called shards, each
managed by a separate machine. Each shard is

tesponsible for indexing a specific portion of the
document collection.

By employing parallel and distributed computing
techniques, Google effectively handle th
enormous scale of the web and provide fast respons
times for search queries. Thousands of computers wor
together to create and maintain the search inde
ensuring that users receive search results quickly a7

‘of documents. This distributed approach is essential

(©) Distribution

8 crucial technique in search engine

chitectures that involves distributing indexes across

multiple computers and/or multiple sites. This approach is

for enabling fast query processing, particularly

9 with large numbers of documents. Index

otters several variations and strategies to
y handle the enormous scale of data in modem

Some of the key aspects of index

Faster Query Processing: Distributing the index across
multiple machines allows for parallel processing of
arch queries. When a user enters a search query. it

be simultaneously processed by multiple machines.
each responsible for a portion of the index. This paralle!

processin

significantly reduces query response times,
enabling fast and real-time search results.

Document Distribution: One approach to index
distribution is document distribution. In this strategy,
the document collection is partitioned and distributed
across multiple machines. Each machine is responsible
for indexing a specific subset of documents. When a
search query it is sent to all relevant
mi documents related to the

s received,
chines that hold the

query's terms.

Term Distribution: Another approach is term
distribution, where the index is divided based on
individual terms. Each machine is responsible for

maintaining the index for specific terms. When a query
is received. it is directed to the machines holding the
index for the relevant terms in the query.

Replication: To ensure fault tolerance and high
availability, index distribution may involve replication.
In replication, copies of the index are maintained on
multiple machines or sites. If one machine or site
becomes unavailable, the search engine can quickly
switch to an alternative location, ensuring continuous
service and minimizing downtime.

Peer-to-Peer (P2P) and uted Information
Retrieval (IR): P2? and distributed IR involve search
across multiple sites or nodes that collaborate in the
search process. P2P systems enable search without the
need for centralized servers by leveraging the
computing power and resources of participating nodes
in the network

Load Balancing: Index distribution also involves load
balancing, where search queries are intelligently

Query Language for Complex Queries: To suppon

Finding Various Morphological Forms of Words:
Morphological forms refer to dierent inflections or
word variations that share the same base or root. For
example, variations of a word may include plurals. ver
tenses, or differer al forms. By identifying
and including various morphological forms of the
terms in the criginal query, the search engine increases
jieving relevant documents that may

gramme

the chances of ret
contain these variations.

Example: Original Query: “run” Query Expansion: “run
(OR runs OR running OR ran”

Searching for Synonyms and Morphological Forms:
This method combines both synonym identification
and various morphological forms discovery. It involves
searching for synonyms of the query terms and also
expanding the query to include different
‘morphological forms of those terms

Example: Orginal Query: “happy” Query Expansion
“nappy OR joyful OR delighted OR happiness OR
happily OR happier”

The goa! cf query expansion is to capture a broader set
Of relevant documents that might contain similar or
related content. even if they do not exactly match the
user's original query terms. By including synonyms and
various morphological forms, search engines improve
recall (the ability to retrieve relevant documents) and
enhance the overall search experience for users

K's worth noting that query expansion is not always
performed automatically by all search engines. In some
cases, users may have the option to enable or disable
avery expansion, depending on their search
preferences. Additionally, query expansion techniques
may vary based on the search engine's algorithms and
the language or domain being searched

(0) Relevance Feedback

Relevance Feedback is a crucial aspect of search engine
user interfaces that involves gathering feedback from
users to improve the relevance of search results
Relevance feedback helps the engine
understand the user's information needs better and
refine the search results accordingly. There are different
types of
feedback is collected

search

relevance feedback based on how the

Explicit Feedback: Explicit feedback is obtained when
a user explicitly indicates the relevance of a document
retrieved for their query. This feedback is usually in the
form of a user's response, such as a “thumbs up" or

‘thumbs down” icon next to search results, allowing
users to vote on the relevance of individual documents.
Users may also have the option to provide more
detailed feedback, such as rating the document's
relevance on a scale of 1 tS

Implicit Feedback: Implicit feedback is inferred from
User behavior during the search process. Instead of the
User explicitly indicating the relevance of a document,

the search engine infers relevance based on the user's
actions. Some examples of implicit feedback include:

<

Ranking
Ranking is a core component of search engines and plays 2
critical role in determining the order in which search results
ate presented to users. The ranking process involves
calculating scores for documents based on a ranking
algorithm, which is the heart of the search engine's retrieval
system. Here are the key aspects of ranking in search
engines

(5.19)

WED RETRIEVAL AND WEB CRAWLING.

* Scoring: The ranking process assigns scores to
documents based on their relevance to the user's
Guery. Each document receives a score that represents
Ms estimated relevance to the query. The higher the
score, the more relevant the document is considered to
be

Ranking Algorithm: The ranking algorithm is 2
mathematical formula that calculates the scores for
documents based on various factors, such as the
Presence of query terms in the document. the
frequency of query terms, and other relevance signals
The algorithm takes into account the importance of
each query term and the significance of its occurrence
in the document
* Query and Document Term Weights: In the scoring
Process, each term in the query (qi) and document (4)
is assigned weight that represents its importance in
the calculation of the document's score. These term
Weights determine how much influence each term has
€on the overall relevance score of the document
* Variations of Ranking Algorithms: There
numerous variations of ranking algorithms and retrieval

are

models used in search engines. Different algorithms
take different factors into account and use various
mathematical techniques to calculate document scores
Examples of ranking algorithms include TF-IOF, 8M25,
language models, and learning-to-rank models.

Ranking is at the

= Core Component of Search Engine:
core of search engine functionality. After the search
engine retrieves a set of candidate documents that
match the user's query, the ranking process determines
the order in which those documents are presented in
the search results. This is crucial for providing users
with the most relevant and valuable information based.
fon their search queries,

Ranking is a

‘Ongoing Research Area in IR:
continuously evolving area of research in Information
Retrieval (IR). Researchers are constantly working on
developing new and more sophisticated ranking
algorithms that improve the accuracy and effectiveness
of search engines. The goal is to enhance the retrieval
quality and deliver better search experiences for users

Ranking Performance Optimization

+ Designing Efficient Ranking Algorithms: ffficient
ranking algorithms are designed to calculate document
scores quickly and accurately. These algorithms
consider various factors such as term frequencies,

===== PDF page_38
+ Term-at-a-Time

algorithms stike a balance between accuracy and
computational complexity.

vs. Document-at-
ranking optimization, the choice
between term-at-a-time and document-at-a-time
processing affects efficiency. Term-at-a-time
Processing scores documents by considering all query
terms at once, whereas document-at-a-time processing
scores documents one term at a time. The choice
depends on factors like the index structure, query
complexity, and hardware resources

Safe vs. Unsafe Optimizations: In the context of
ranking, safe optimizations maintain the integrity of the
ranking algorithm and ensure that relevant documents
are not excluded due to optimizations. Unsafe
optimizations may introduce false positives or false
negatives in the results. Search engines prioritize safe
optimizations to maintain result quality.

Distribution: Distribution is a key strategy to handle
large-scale search queries and document collections
efficiently. By distributing the search and ranking tasks
across multiple machines or nodes, search engines can
parallelize processing and reduce response times for

Caching as Distributed Searching: Caching is a form
of distributed searching where search results are stored
temporarily in cache memory. When a query matches 9
cached result, the search engine can quickly retrieve
the pre-computed result from the cache, reducing the
need for re-computation and improving response
times,

optimization technique in distributed systems to
ensure that resources are evenly utilized across nodes.

It prevents overloading of specific nodes, maximizing
overall system efficiency.

index through compression reduces _ storage

requirements and speeds up query processing. Various

Cluster Based Architecture

Custer-based architecture is a popular approach used in
web retrieval systems to improve efficiency and scalability
In this architecture, the search engine distributes. the
workload across multiple machines or nodes, known as
clusters, to handle the enormous scale of the web and
accommodate a large number of users and queries. Each
cluster operates independently, processing a subset of the
data and search requests. The key features of a cluster-
based web retrieval architecture are as follows:

+ Distributed Indexing and Searcl The web
retrieval system partitions the index and document

/EB RETRIEVAL AND VICE CRAVING

esponsible for storing and manag)

Used to route incorning queries to the anarap!
Clusters based on their capacity and load

Parallel Processing: Cluster-based architectures allow
for parallel processing of search queries. Each cluster
Can independently process the queries assigned to
Which significantly improves the overall search spe
and response time.

Fault Tolerance and Redundancy: By replicating
index data and distributing it across multiple clusters,
the architecture provides fault tolerance and

redundancy If one cluster becomes unavailable oF
‘experiences issues, the load can be redistributed to
‘ther healthy clusters to ensure continuous service
Scalability: Cluster-based architectures are highly
Scalable, as additional clusters can be added to the
system to accommodate growing data and query
volumes. This allows search engines to handle @ vast
number of web pages and users effectively

Query Routing and Result Merging: A query router
directs incoming queries to the appropriate clusters
based on the query terms. After processing the queries
the results from all clusters are merged and ranked to
form the final search results presented to the user.

Index Distribution: The index is divided into smaller
segments, and each cluster maintains a subset of the
index. This index distribution helps reduce the memory
‘and storage requirements for individual clusters
MapReduce Framework: Some cluster-based web
retrieval systems leverage MapReduce or similar
distributed computing frameworks to facilitate parallel
processing and efficient data analysis.

Google, one of the world's largest search engines, uses 2

cluster-based architecture to handle the vast number of

how the cluster-based architecture works in Google's web

===== PDF page_39
Indexing and Document Distribution: Google's web

nuously crawl the web, discovering and
new web pages. The crawled data is
Processed and divided into smaller segments known as
shards. Each shard contains a subset of the web pages
and is stored on a separate server or node

Indexing

Cluster Formation: Google’s data centers comprise
thousands of individual servers, organized into clusters
Each cluster consists of multiple machines working

together to handle search requests and store index
data

Load Balancing and Query Routing: When 2 user
submits a search query on the Google search engine.
the query is directed to a load balancer. The load
bolancer's role is to distribute the incoming queries
evenly across multiple clusters, ensuring that no single
cluster is overwhelmed with requests.

Parallel Query Processing: Once a query is routed t0
2 specific cluster, the cluster independently processes
the query in parallel. Each shard in the cluster searches

its index for matching documents related to the query
terms

Result Merging and Ranking: After processing the
query, each shard generates a set of search results, The
results from all the shards within the cluster are
merged, and a final ranking of documents is computed
based on various relevance factors, including the
number of query term occurrences, link analysis, and
other relevance signals,

Result Presentation: The ranked search results are
presented to the user, usually as a list of web page
links on the search results page. Each link is
accompanied by a snippet of text from the
corresponding web page to provide a preview of the
page's content,

‘+ Fault Tolerance and Redundancy: Google's cluster-
based architecture ensures fault tolerance and
redundancy by replicating index data across multiple
servers and data centers. If one cluster or server
experiences an issue, the load can be redirected to
other healthy clusters, ensuring continuous service.

By employing a cluster-based architecture, Google is able
to efficiently process billions of web pages and millions of
search queries every day. The distributed nature of the
architecture allows Google to handle the enormous scale of
the web, provide fast response times, aud deliver highly
relevant search results to its users worldwide,

Brocessing the order on the main web server, the task

4 Search Engine Ranking

Search engine ranking is the process of determining the
relevance and order of search results in response to 2
User's query. When a user enters a search query. the search

engine's ranking algorithm evaluates. the indexed.

documents to identify the most relevant ones and presents

them to the user in 2 ranked list. The goal of search engine

ranking is to provide users with the most valuable and

relevant results for their queries

Key Aspects of Search Engine Ranking:

= Ranking Algorithm: The ranking algorithm is the core
component of a search engine's ranking process. It
Uses various factors and. signals to calculate the
relevance of documents to the user's query. The
algorithm considers elements like keyword frequency.
document quality, user feedback, link analysis, and
other relevance metrics

INFORMATION RETRIEVAL
+ Relevance Signals: Search engines use numerous

documents. These signals include term frequency (how

often query terms appear in a document), document
quality authority and trustworthiness of the
and user behavior (eg. click-through rates

Iterative Ranking: Search engines often use iterative

As users interact with search results, the ranking

+ Evaluating Rani

1g Effectiveness: Search engines
continuously evaluate the effectiveness of their ranking

allows search engines to refine and optimize their
algorithms over time

Anti-Spam Measures: Search engines implement anti:
spam measures to identify and penalize low-quality or
manipulative content. This helps ensure that search
results are trustworthy and relevant to users.

Iterative Calculation: The PageRank algorithm is aq
iterative process. Initially, all pages are assigned y
default PageRank score. In cach iteration, the algorithm
recaleulates the PageRank scores of pages based on
the current scores of their linking pages. This process
continues until the PageRank scores converge to stable
values

k Graph: To implement link-based ranking, search
engines construct a link graph that represents the
relationships between web pages based on the links
Each page is represented as a node, and the links
between pages are represented as edges in the graph

Link-Based Relevance: Link-based ranking
complements traditional content-based ranking
methods (such as keyword analysis) by providing a way
to assess the authority and importance of pages based
on their relationships with other pages.

Kc Spam and Manipulation: Link-based ranking is
susceptible to manipulation, and some website owners
have attempted to artificially inflate their PageRank
scores through link schemes and link farms. To counter
this, search engines continuously update their
algorithms and use anti-spam measures to identify and
penalize manipulative link practices

The HITS algorithm starts with an initial set of hub and

authority scores for all web pages in the network Then, it

Meratively updates these scores until they converge to

stable values. In each iteration, the hub score of each page
5 recalculated based on the authority scores of the pages it
links 0, and the authority score of each page is

recalculated based on the hub scores of the pages that link
toit

The iterative process con

Jes until the hub and authority
scores no longer change significantly. At this point, the
algorithm has identified the hubs and authorities in the
network

Applications of HITS:

search results. By identifying authoritative pages

HITS helps improve the quality of search results,

Key Steps of the PageRank Algorithm:

«Graph Representation: The web pages and their ting,
 ropepresented as a directed graph, where each page
ee made, and the links between pages ae representeg
as edges

«Initial Score Assignment: Initially, each page
assigned an equal PageRank score. In the early stageg
Of the algorithm, all pages are considered to have the
‘same level of importance.

+ Iterative Calculation: The PageRank algorithm then
iteratively updates the PageRank scores of each page
In each iteration, the PageRank score of a page is
recalculated based on the PageRank scores of the
pages that link to it

+ Weighted Contributions: The contribution of a linking

page’s PageRank to a linked page's PageRank is
proportional to the linking page's own PageRank and
the number of outbound links it has. Pages with higher
PageRank scores pass more authority to the pages they
link to,

To get over these two problems, we introduce the notion
of teleportation.

Teleportation consists of connecting each node of the
graph to all other nodes. The graph will be then complete
The idea is with 2 certain probability B, the random walker
will jump to another node according to the transition
matrix P and with a probability (1-B)/n, it will jump
randomly to any node in the graph. We get then the new
transition matrix R

R= BP +(-Byve

where v is a vector of ones, and e a vector of 1/n

B is commonly defined as the damping factor. In practice, it

is advised to set B to 0.85.

‘term Frequency-Inverse
(TF-IDF):
TF-IOF is @ widely used ranking function that calculates
the importance of a term in 2 document relative to >
collection of documents. it is based on two factors
Tecm Frequency (TF) and lnverse Document Frequency
(DF). TF measures the frequency of a term in 6
document, while IDF measures how rare or common
the term is across the entire document collection. The
TF-IDF score of a term in a document is the product of
its TF and IDF scores.
