[5.4 WEB CRAWLER \

A web crawiler, also known 25 2 s2'¢

20 robot, or wl
bot. is an automated program or scriot that systemat
browses the internet and collects information from wel
ages. Web crawlers are a fundamental component ¢
search engines, helping to index and gather data from
vast number of wi

BM25 is an extension of the TF-IDF ranking function
that introduces additional parameters to control the
term weighting and document length normalization. It
is considered an improvement over the traditional TF-
IDF model for better search result ranking,

Evaluation Metric:

Stes, making it accessible throus

search queries. Here's how web crawlers work

* Starting Point: A web crawler begins its journey fre
a list of seed URLs, which are typically 2 small set
known and trusted web pages. These seed URLs s¢
{as the starting points for the crawler to navigate
‘explore the web.

a. Precision:
Precision

measures the proportion of relevant
documents

among the retrieved documents. It
indicates how well the search engine is performing in
returning only the relevant results to the user's query. It

Fetching Web Pages: The crawler sends HTTP reat
to the seed URLs and retrieves the corresponding
pages’ HTML content. The crawler identifies the

‘of URLS awaiting proce:

as a queue or stack

Following Links: The crawler visits the URLs from the

Politeness and Crawling &
need to be

avoid 0}

jwette: Web crawlers
respectful of web servers’ resources and
venshelming them with too many requests in a
short period. Politeness rules, such as obeying
robots tat files (which instruct crawlers on which parts
of the website to crawl and which to avoid) and
adhering to crawl rate limits, help crawlers maintain
good crawling etiquette.

Duplicate URL Management: Web crawlers. often
encounter duplicate URLs leading to the same content.
To avoid redundant crawling and index storage, they
employ duplicate URL management techniques to
identify and handle duplicate URLs efficiently.

Indexing and Storing Data: As the web crawler
fetches web pages, it extracts and stores relevant data
from the pages, such as text content, metadata, links,
and other information. This data is then indexed and
organized to enable efficient search and retrieval by
the search engine.

Continuous Crawling: Web crawlers are continuously
active, as the web is continually changing with new
content being published and old content being
updated or removed. Therefore, crawlers revisit
previously crawled web pages to detect changes and
update their indexes accordingly.

5.4.1 Web Crawler Structure

The structure of a web crawler typically consists of several

‘components

that work together to efficiently and

systematically browse the internet and collect data from
web pages. Here’s an overview of the main components in
the structure of a web crawler:

Seed URLs: The web crawler starts its journey from a
list of seed URLs. These seed URLs act as the initial
entry points to the web, from which the crawler begins
its crawling process

URL Deduplication: URL deduplication ensures tha,
the same web page is not crawled multiple times if iy
appears with different URLS. This component prevents
redundancy and optimizes the crawling process
Indexer: The indexer is responsible for organizing ang
storing the collected data from web pages in
Ftructured manner. It prepares the data for efficient
Search and retrieval by building an index that facilitates
‘quick access to relevant information.

Scheduler: The scheduler controls the order in which
URls are fetched and processed by the crawler. It
prioritizes URLs based on factors like relevance,
freshness, importance, or any specific crawling policies.

Politeness Policy: The politeness policy enforces crawl
rate limits to ensure that the web crawler does not
overload web servers with too many requests in a short
period. It follows the guidelines set by the website
owners to maintain good crawling etiquette.

Crawl Control and Throttling: Craw! control and
throttling mechanisms manage the rate at which the
web crawler accesses and downloads web pages. These
mechanisms prevent excessive crawling and ensure the
crawler operates within the website's resource limits.

The interaction and coordination of these components in
the web crawler structure enable it to efficiently traverse
the web, collect data from web pages, and build a

comprehensive and up-to-date index of the internet, The

Fig. 5.9: Web crawler architecture
web Crawler Architecture refers to the design and
structure of a program that automatically browses the
iweb for information. The architecture of a web cranle
ig responsible for defining how the crawier functions,
wnat it does, and how it interacts with the websites 4
visits.
‘at a high level, a web crawler has three main
components: the front end, the back end, and the
storage system. The front end is the user interface
where the user inputs the initial URL and specifies what
information they want to extract,
he back end is responsible for performing the actual
web crawling process and consists of multiple modules
such as a URL scheduler, a downloader, and a parser.
The URL scheduler is responsible for keeping track of
which URLs have been visited and deciding which URLs
should be visited next.
The downloader retrieves the HTML content of the web
pages and passes it to the parser. The parser then
extracts the relevant information and stores it in the
storage system.
The storage system is responsible for keeping track of
the information that has been extracted by the parser
and organizing it in a way that is easily accessible and
searchable. This can be a database or a fie system
A well-designed web cravler architecture is crucial to
the success of a web crawling project. It determines
how the web crawler operates, what information it can
extract, and how that information can be stored and

retrieved.

Apache Nutch: Apache Nutch is an open-source web
Crawler and search engine written in Java. It is designed
for scalable crawling and indexing of the wed Nutch
supports distributed crawling, enabling developers to
deploy it on large-scale clusters for handling massive
crawling tasks. It is widely used in enterprise-level
search and information retrieval systems,

Heritrixc Heritrix is another open-source web crawler
‘written in Java. It is developed by the Internet Archive
‘and designed specifically for archiving web content.
Heritrix supports a wide range of customization
options and provides sophisticated crawling control,
enabling developers to tailor it for various crawling
needs.
Goutte: Goutte is a web scraping and crawling library
for PHP. It is built on top of the Guzzle HTTP library
and provides a straightforward interface for crawing
web pages and extracting data. Goutte is useful for
developers working with PHP-based web applications.
Puppeteer: Puppeteer is a Nodejs library developed
by Google. It is not specifically designed for web
crawling but is commonly used for automating browser
tasks, such as navigating web pages and scraping data

These web crawler libraries offer various features and

capabilities suited to different crawling tasks and

Programming languages. Developers can choose the most

appropriate library based on their project requirements,

Programming language preference, and the complexity of

the crawling tasks they need to accomplish

Python Scrapy

Scrapy is a fast, open-source web crawling framework

written in Python, used to extract the data from the web

page with the help of selectors based on XPath

Scrapy was first released on June 26, 2008 licensed under

8SD, with a milestone 1.0 releasing in June 2015.

Why Use Serapy?

+ tis easier to build and scale large crawling projects

+ It has a built-in mechanism called Selectors, for

‘extracting the data from websites.

+ Ithandles the requests asynchronously and itis fast.

It automatically adjusts crawling speed using Auto-

throttling mechanism,

+ Ensures developer accessibility

Features of Serapy

+ Scrapy is an open source and free to use web crawling
framework.

+ Scrapy generates feed exports in formats such as JSON,
SV, and XML.

+ Scrapy has built-in support for selecting and extracting
data from sources either by XPath or CSS expressions.

+ Scrapy based on crawler, allows extracting data from
the web pages automatically.

Advantages

+ Scrapy is easily extensible, fast, and powerful

* Its a cross-platform application framework (Windows,
Unux, Mac OS and BSD),

+ Scrapy requests are
asynchronously

scheduled and processed

+ Scrapy comes with builtsin service called Serapyd
which allows to upload projects and control spiders
using JSON web service.

Its possible to scrap any website, though that website
does not have API for raw data access

Running Beautiful Soup

Its time to test our Beautiful Soup package in one of the
html pages (taking web page:

information from it.

the webpage:
from bs4 import BeautifulSoup
import requests
url = "https://www.tutorialspoint.com/index.htm"

req = requests.get(url)

soup = BeautifulSoup(req,text, "html.parser")

print(soup.title)

5.4.4 Applications of Web Crawlers

Web crawlers have 2 wide range of applications across
various domains and industries. They play a crucial role in
collecting, indexing, and organizing data from the internet,
making it accessible for different purposes. Here are some
‘common applications of web crawlers:

+ Search Engines: Web crawlers are the backbone of

search engines like Google, Bing, and Yahoo. They
continuously crawl the web, collecting information
from web pages, and build comprehensive indexes of
web content. These indexes enable search engines to

Provide relevant search results to users when they
enter queries,

Content Aggregation: Web crawlers are used by

research,

s multimedia Information Retrieval (MIR) is a field that
Meals with the retrieval organization, and analysis of
multimedi2 data. It encompasses a wide range of
memniques and technologies that enable users to
weoych, access, and analyze multimedia content
efficiently:

multimedia data refers to any information that involves
multiple media types, such as text, images, audio,
fideo, and other forms of data. With the explosive
rowth of digital content on the internet and various
frultimedia platforms, effective retrieval of multimedia
gata has become essential for various applications,
including search engines, content recommendation
aystems, digital libraries, and more

yey components of Multimedia Information Retrieval:
pata Representation: Representing multimedia data
jin a form suitable for retrieval is a crucial step. For
instance, images and videos can be represented as
feature vectors oF histograms, while audio can be
represented as spectrograms or
Cepstral Coefficients (MCCS)

Mel-Frequency

Indexing and Retrieval: To enable efficient searching,
multimedia databases need to be indexed. Indexing
involves creating structures that facilitate the retrieval
of relevant data quickly. Various indexing techniques
like inverted files, tree-based indexing, and hashing are
used to speed up the search process

+ Content-Based Retrieval: In content-based retrieval,
the system finds multimedia content similar to a query
based on its visual, auditory, or textual features rather
than relying on metadata or annotations. This is
commonly used for searching images and videos based
‘on their visual similarity.

* Text-Based Retrieval: While content-based retrieval is

Useful for visual and auditory media, text-based
retrieval involves using keywords and text-based
descriptions to retrieve multimedia content that
matches the query.

IR APPLICATIONS

Multimedia Analysis: Techniques ike ob
‘cognition, speech recognition, emotion analysis and
Seine “detection are used to enract. meaningful

information from multimedia date, making t easier to
index and retrieve

* Relevance Feedback: In some cases, users’ feedback is

collected to refine search results. For example,
Searches for images of “beaches”
include some irrelevant images. ti

if auser
‘and the results

e user can provide:
feedback to improve subsequent searches

* Evaluation Metrics

Evaluating the pedormance of

multimedia information retrieval systems is essential to
assess their effectiveness. Metrics like precision, recall
and F-measure are commonly used {6r evaluation.

Challenges in Multimedia Information Retrieval:

* Large-Scale Data: With the vast amount of multimedia

data available online, managing and efficiently

searching through large-scale databases can be

challenging

* Heterogeneous Data: Multimedia data comes in
various formats, making it complex to handle different
‘media types in a unified retrieval system

‘+ Feature Extraction: Extracting meaningful features
from multimedia data that accurately represent its

content is a complex task.

* Semantic Gap: There is often a disconnect between
low-level features extracted from multimedia data and
high-level semantic understanding, making relevance
determination challenging

* User Intent Understan
intent while formulating 2 query and delivering

Understanding the user's

relevant results remains a challenging problem.

Multimedia Information Retrieval is an active and
evolving field, with ongoing and
advancements aiming to address these challenges and
improve the efficiency and effectiveness of multimedia

search and analysis systems

{6.1.1 Spoken Language Audio Retrieval

Spoken Language Audio Retrieval, also known as Spoken
Term Detection (STD) or Spoken Term Recognition (STR). is
2 specific task in the field of multimedia information
retrieval that focuses on finding occurrences of specific
spoken words or -phrases within audio recordings. It
involves searching through a large collection of. spoken
language audio data to identity and retrieve segments that
contain the target spoken term or keyword.

The Task of Spoken Language Audio Retrieval
Challenging Due to Several Factor

Speech Recognition: First, the system needs to
convert the audio data into text through Automatic
Speech Recognition (ASR) techniques. ASR aims to
transcribe spoken language into its corresponding
textual representation. The quality and accuracy of ASR
Play a crucial role in the overall effectiveness of Spoken
Language Audio Retrieval.

Keyword Spotting: Once the audio is transcribed, the
system performs keyword spotting to identify the
occurrences of the target spoken word or phrase.
Keyword spotting focuses on locating the specific
terms in the audio and extracting the relevant
segments containing those keywords.

Large-scale Data: Spoken language audio databases
can be extensive, and efficient search algorithms are
required to handle the large volume of data effectively.

+ Noisy and Real-world Data: Spoken language audio

recordings often come with various challenges like
background noise, accents, speech variations, and
environmental conditions, which can impact the
accuracy of both ASR and keyword spotting.

* Vocabulary and Language Modeling: Proper

vocabulary and language modeling are essential to
handle different word pronunciations, variations, and
language-specific characteristics.

Applications of Spoken Language Audio Retrieval
Include:

+ Voice search in audi

or video content
+ Multimedia
keywords

content retrieval based on spoken

* Content-based ad targeting in audio and video streams

Building voice assistants and voice-controlled systems

Audio Feature Extraction: Converting the audio signal

the audio to_text() function returns the recognized text, or

© Similarity Matching: Calculating the similarity

database to rank the retrieved results

Simple example of non-speech audio retrieval using
Python In this example, we will use the Librosa library to
extract audio features and the NearestNeighbors algorithm
from scikitlearn to perform similarity matching for
retrieval, We will focus on retrieving audio segments with
specific musical instruments.

Before running the code, make sure you have installed the
required libraries:

ip install librosa

pip install scikit-learn
For this example, we assume you have a directory
containing audio files with various musical instruments.

Let's name this directory “audio_dataset” and assume it
contains audio files in WAV format.

(b) Graph Retrieval
Including:

Sipe, 1s 8 specialized area of information retieval that
fecuses on searching. and retrieving information from
gaphrstrctured data In this contest, 2 graph consis of
Roges representing entities or objects and edges
representing relationships or connections between these
entities. Graph retrieval is commonly used in various
domains where data is represented in a graph format, such
as social networks, knowledge graphs, biological networks,
and citation networks.

has Numerous

Applications,

+ Keyword-based search in knowled

graohs
+ Social network analysis and community detection
+ Identifying

networks.

relevant scientific

Citation

(@) Graph Retrieval Involves Two Key Components:

Recommender systems based on user-item interaction
1. Query Specification: The user specifies a query in the omnis
form of a graph pattern or a subgraph, representing
the information they are interested in retrieving, The
query can be a simple subgraph with specific nodes
and edges or a more complex graph pattern
representing a specific structure.

‘The field of graph retrieval is an active area of research, and
ongoing advancements in graph date represen
indexing techniques,

and. graph matching
continue to improve the efficiency and effectiveness of
graph retrieval systems.

To create 9 Python example of graph retrieval, we will use
NetworkX. 3 popular Python library for working with graph
data In this example, we will demonstrate how to perform
3 simple graph retrieval task by searching for subgraphs

that match 2 specific query pattern in a small graph
database.

Betore running the code, make sure you have installed
Nenworkx:

pip install nenvork

follows:

import networkx as nx

def graph_retrieval(graph_database, query_pat tern):
# Initialize a list to store the matching subgraphs
matching_subgraphs = (]

# Lterate through all nodes in the graph database to
find matching subgraphs

for node in graph_database.nodes()
# Get the subgraph centered at the current node

subgraph = nx.ego_graph(graph_database, node,
radius=1, center=Teue)

# Check if the subgraph matches the query
pattern

if nx.is_isomorphic(subgraph, query_pat tern):
‘matching_subgraphs.oppend(subgraph)

return matching_subgraphs

The graph retrieval() function iterates through all nodes in
the graph database and extracts subgraphs centered at
‘each node with a given radius, It then checks if the

subgraph matches the query pattern using NetworkX’s

is isomorphic) function, which checks if two graphs are
isomorphic (ie, structurally identical

In the maind function, we call the graph.rettievall) function
with the graph database and the query pattem. The
function returns a list of matching subgraphs, and we print
them to the console

Please note that this is a simple illustrative example. In real-
world scenarios, larger graph databases, more complex
query patterns, and more sophisticated graph matching

techniques would be used for practical graph retrieval
tasks.

In this example, we define a simple color histogram feature
extraction method using OpenCV. The extract feature()
function takes an image path as input, loads the image,
resizes it, and computes its color histogram. The histogram
is then normalized and flattened to obtain the feature
vector.

The image_retrievall) function performs imagery retrieval
by calculating the cosine similarity between the query
image and each image in the database directory. The top-k

similar images are then retrieved and printed to the
console

= Workflow of a Video Retrieval Recommender
System: The workflow of a video retrieval
recommender system typically involves the following
steps
1. User Profiling: Collecting information about the
user's preferences, interests, and past video
interactions to build a user profile.
2. Video Representation: Extracting visual features
from videos to represent their content,

© Recommender Sy:

filtering,

3. Query Formulation: Formulating user queries
based on their preferences or the content of a
selected video

4. Content-Based Video Retrieval: Using content-
based video retrieval techniques to find videos that
are visually similar to the query video or the user's
preferences.

Video Content Recommendation in E-Learnin

Advancements: Advancements in deep learning,
paticularly in video understanding and feature extraction
using Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs), have
significantly improved the performance of video retrieval
recommender systems. Pre-trained CNN models, such as
BO (Inflated 3D ConvNet), can be fine-tuned for video
feature extraction, leading to more effective content-based
retrieval and recommendation.

interactions

Measure user Similarity: Calculate the similarity
Peween the target user and all other users based on
their interactions with items. Common similarity metrics
include cosine similarity, Pearson correlation, and
yaccard similarity.

select Top Similar Users: Identify the top-k most
similar users to the target user.

predict Ratings: Predict the target user’s ratings for
items they have not interacted with by taking weighted
averages of ratings given by the top-k similar users
ttem-Based Collaborative Filtering: In this approach,
the system recommends items to a target user based
on the preferences of other users who have interacted
with similar items. The similarity between items is
measured based on user-item interactions. The steps

involved in item-based collaborative filtering are as
follows:

> Build a User-Item Matrix: Create a matrix where
rows represent users, columns represent items, and
the matrix elements

So How to Recommend an Item to the User?
Lets suppose we have to recommend new items to user10,
and we know a userl0 already likes/watch item7,8,1. Now
ME GO fo the item-item similarity matrix, we take the most
Similar item to items7.8.1 based on the similarity values

lets suppose the most similar item for item? is {item9,

‘tems, item10}, the Most similar item to items is {item19,

item 4, iteml0} and the Most similar item to item 1 is
(items, item24, item10)

Now we take a very common item from every set of items
and the common items are {item9, itemé, item10, item 19,
item 14) and we recommend these all items to user10.

The most popular filtering is item item-based filtering

because over time item is not changed like the user user-
based similarity.

Collaborative Filtering has Several Advantages

1k does not require item features or content information,
‘making it applicable to a wide range of recommendation
scenarios.

It can provide personalized recommendations based on
user behavior and preferences.

It can handle new items and users without needing explicit
information about them,

However, Collaborative

Disadvantages:

Filtering also has Some

Cold Start Problem: Collaborative filtering struggles
to make accurate recommendations for new users or
items with limited interaction data,

Sparsity: In large datasets, user-item interactions may
be sparse, leading to challenges in finding meaningful
User or item similarities.

Scalability: As the number of users and items grows,
calculating user or item similarities can become

computationally expensive.

To address these limitations, hybrid approaches that
combine collaborative filtering with other recommendation
techniques, such as content-

ased filtering or matrix
factorization, are often used to build more robust and
accurate recommender systems.

search query in search engine like google, @ count
representation might be more appropriate.

attribute = 1/(3) = 0.577.

Personalization and Reasoning

Knowledge-based recommendation —systerns can

knowledge and rules

handie the cold start problem by using explicit
knowledge and rules to generate recommendations for
new users or items

In this example, the extract_movie_info() function processes
the text data using the NLTK library. It tokenizes the text
into words and performs part-of-speech (POS) tagging to
identify word types. It then extracts movie names and their
corresponding genres from the text.
Please note that this is a simple example for illustration
purposes. In real-world applications, more sophisticated
natural language processing techniques and Named Entity
Recognition (NER) methods can be used to handle complex
text data and extract a broader range of information for
knowledge-based recommendation systems.

6.4 SEMANTIC WEB ]

‘+The Semantic Web is an extension of the World Wide
Web that aims to make web content more easily
understandable and accessible to both humans and
machines. It is based on the idea of adding semantic
meaning to web data, allowing information to be

Inference engines and

Advantages and Applications of the Semantic Web:

Data Integration: The Semantic Web enables the
integration of data from different sources, making it
easier to link and combine information.
Machine-Readable Content: By adding semantic
meaning to web data, machines can better understand
and process the information, leading to more
intelligent applications.

(6.5 COLLECTING AND INTEGRATING

* Web Scrapin:

SPECIALIZED INFORMATION ON THE

WEB
Collecting and integrating specialized information from the
web involves gathering data from various web sources that
focus on a specific domain or topic, The process requires
web scraping, data extraction, and data integration
techniques to aggregate and combine data from multiple
sources into a unified dataset. Here's a general outline of
the steps involved
+ Identify Specialized Sources: Determine the web
sources that provide specialized information relevant
to your domain or topic of interest. These sources may
include websites, online databases, forums, social
media platforms, and other online resources,

Use web scraping techniques to extract
data from the identified web sources. Web scraping
involves parsing the HTML content of web pages to
extract relevant data, such as text, images, links, and
structured data

ICASE STUDY: DEMONSTRATE.
COLLABORATIVE FILTERING USING ANY

DATASETS TO RECOMMEND ITEMS TO
USERS

For this demonstration, let's assume we have a dataset
where users have rated movies. We'll use this dataset to
recommend movies to a specific user based on the
preferences of similar users.

Dataset:

Here's a simplified example of the movie ratings dataset:

rated movies on a scale from y
In this example, users have 12 a 5

to 5, wth "1" being the lowest rating and "5" being the
: indicates that a user hasn't rated g

highest rating
particular movie.

Steps:

1. User Similarity Calculation:

© Calculate the similarity between users based on the)
movie ratings. Common similarity measures include
cosine similarity or Pearson correlation.

© For instance, you can calculate the cosine similarity
between User 1 and User 2 based on their ratings for
Movie 8 and Movie D.

2. Find Similar Users:

+ Identify users who are similar to the target user (for
whom you want to make recommendations)

* Let's assume User 3 is our target user, and we find that
User 1 and User 4 are similar to User 3.

3. Item Recommendation:

+ Recommend items (movies) to the target user based
on what the similar users have liked.

+ For example, if User 1 and User 4 have rated Movie A
highly and User 2 has rated Movie C highly, we can
recommend Movie A and Movie C to User 3.

Outcome: .

In this example, collaborative filtering recommends Movie

A and Movie C to User 3 based on the preferences of

similar users (User 1 and User 4 for Movie A, and User 2 for

Movie ©).

In practice, collaborative filtering algorithms scale to much

larger datasets and use more sophisticated methods to

calculate user similarities and generate recommendations.

Common techniques include user-based collaborative

filtering and item-based collaborative filtering. Additionally,

matrix factorization methods and machine learning models
can be applied to enhance recommendation accuracy.
